@ @ @ @ @ @ @ this week in chemistry history , Alice Augusta Ball was born , one of the first African-American women to earn an advanced degree in chemistry and who developed the first injectable treatment for leprosy . <p> Ball was born in Seattle , Washington on July 24th , 1892 . <p> She was incredibly academic , earning top grades in the sciences early on . <p> She went on to obtain 2 degrees , one in pharmaceutical chemistry and one in pharmacy , and published her research on benzylations in ether solution in the prestigious Journal of the American Chemical Society . <p> In 1915 , she completed her master 's in chemistry at the University of Hawaii , writing an impressive thesis on the chemical properties of the kava plant . <p> She was promptly brought on as a chemistry professor , becoming the institution 's first African-American and female professor at the age of just 23 . <p> At this point in history , science was searching for an effective treatment for leprosy . <p> It was a disease surrounded in stigma , causing painful lesions on the face @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lack of real medical knowledge led governments around the world to force people with leprosy into strict exile . <p> In 1865 , the Hawaiian authorities began forcibly removing infected individuals , quarantining them on the island of Molokai , where they remained until they succumbed to the disease . <p> Back then , a leprosy diagnosis was a lonely death sentence . <p> The only available treatment for leprosy was chaulmoogra tree oil , either consumed or rubbed onto the skin . <p> But the oil had mixed success . <p> While it contained active acids that could kill leprosy-causing bacteria , it was hard on the stomach and had a terrible taste , so vile that many refused to take it . <p> And the oil 's high viscosity prevented efficient absorption as an ointment or an injection . <p> In order for the treatment to work , it needed to be modified into a useful form . <p> Ball took on this challenge . <p> She identified the main 2 components , chaulmoogric acid and hydnocarpic acid , and isolated their fatty acids . <p> She then @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The result was a safe and effective injectable treatment for leprosy . <p> The Ball method , as it would be called , helped free countless people from isolated leper colonies . <p> It was n't a cure , but it was as close as anyone had gotten , and her method quickly became the most widely used treatment for leprosy in the pre-antibiotic years of the 1920s and 30s . <p> Unfortunately , Ball died suddenly just a year after her discovery , before she could publish her findings . <p> President of the College of Hawaii , Arthur Dean and chemistry professor Richard Wrenshall , came across her findings and took credit for the discovery , naming the method the Dean Method . <p> It took over a century before the truth came out . <p> In the 1970s , 2 professors at the University of Hawaii had heard rumours of Ball 's work and dug through the university 's archives , eventually finding evidence of the true developer of the groundbreaking treatment . <p> Since then , Ball 's work has been widely recognised . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Medicine honoured Ball by placing her name in the frieze of its main building . <p> And in February 2022 , Governor David Ige of Hawaii declared February 28th Alice Augusta Ball Day in recognition of her discovery . <p> Despite the original miscredit , Ball 's legacy remains as a young pioneer who shattered racial and gender barriers in STEM and saved thousands of lives , paving the way for future leprosy treatment . <h> Outro <p> Mariana Kneppers <p> That 's all for this edition of the podcast . <p> If you 're interested and want to hear more about any of the items we 've @@139199636 <p> Klara McGriskin is not a person to shirk a challenge . And for the future of farming in Ireland , it does n't come much bigger than succession . <p> With the average age of an Irish farmer now 59 and one third over the age of 65 , it 's an issue that the agriculture sector can not hide from , according to Teagasc farm management specialist . Klara is quick to acknowledge that it is a " hugely emotive and sensitive " matter to resolve , but at the same time , the nettle must be grasped . <p> " It 's always been a big issue because every farm goes through it , " the Leitrim native observes , saying her job is to give farmers options , but the decision ultimately rests with the family . " It 's there . It has to be done . Nobody can run away from it . In some cases , it 's not an issue . In some cases , it is . " <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> What is abundantly clear to her is that succession is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is planned for when there are choices rather than leaving it to an emergency situation where there are less and more chances of conflict . <p> You 're not better than them in any way , you 're just there to help them and make a difference so that their lives can be a little bit better <p> She advises farmers firstly to make a will and secondly , start a chat ; even though some hard questions on change may have to be asked and answered . <p> " There are two things that are certain in life : you 're going to die , and there 's going to be a lot of change , " she says matter-of-factly . " But people ca n't handle change , and they really ca n't in an agricultural perspective , and that 's just one thing that people have to understand . " <p> On the dairy side , there is " big labour shortage " currently because " the future generation of farmers do not want to farm the way that our predecessors did . They want a better @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ go down well at home . " <p> As a result , they are not taking on the farms as young as they probably should . <p> Klara , who grew up on a suckler and sheep farm in Manorhamilton , finds many people will speak more readily with a professional than their own family on this issue . Her role covers succession , inheritance , finance , consolidation and business planning from a farm management perspective . <p> " If people come to us for succession help , we help them , but we take our taxation and legal hat off , and we look at the business side . They find that the accountants and solicitors do n't really understand the farm , whereas we are farmers . We 're all trained in ag . We understand farming . " <p> Emotional side <p> Contrary to what people might think , the taxation and legal side is only 20% of her discussion with a farmer . The main 80% is about the emotional side of the process and the adjustment to change . <p> " For the person @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ That 's their identity , " she stresses , something that underlines to her how important it is that every farmer makes time for an interest outside of agriculture . <p> " They do n't have to leave the farm 100% . You can still farm away , just in a different way , but they also need something else to go to an interest . They 're not just going to sit at home at the kitchen table all day . " <p> Having studied agricultural science in UCD , Klara initially thought the environmental route was the career path for her . She worked as a Green , Low-Carbon , Agri-Environment Scheme ( GLAS ) planner with FRS before spending over two years in Gurteen Agricultural College running its part-time and distance courses . One element of this work was business planning . This involved a major focus on succession which she discovered she liked from a business and heritage perspective . <p> She later spent several years as an assistant agricultural inspector in the climate change and renewable energy policy division of the Department of Agriculture . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Abbeyleix , Co Laois , and Klara breeds pedigree New Zealand Suffolks , which are currently one of the top flocks in the country . " That is my love , " she says , smiling . <p> She later moved to Teagasc for several years as a drystock adviser , which she absolutely loved . But it was n't without its challenges , initially . <p> Klara McGriskin , Teagasc farm management specialist . /Justin Lynch <p> Klara recalls being told at her first discussion group that there was no point being there . " A girl , a woman , was not going to come in here and tell me how to farm -- and that was in a group of about 20-plus men . " <p> However , she took a different tack by asking each of them how long they had been farming . When it was added up , it was close to 700 years of agricultural experience in the room . <p> " I said , ' I 've been farming since I was , say , knee-high . So , I said , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so you all know a lot more than me . So , you all need to tell me . I need to learn from you . Well , their perspective just changed completely . " She now considers many of her clients from those days friends . <p> However , it did convince Klara that as a woman in agriculture , you need to stand up for yourself to earn respect . <p> " You 're not better than them in any way , you 're just there to help them and make a difference so that their lives can be a little bit better . <p> Klara also spent time as a Teagasc education officer through Covid in Thurles . Having extensive experience on the advisory and education side , it set her up well for her current job , where one of her primary functions is to train advisers so they can give information to their clients , including how to broach a sensitive topic like succession . <p> Challenges <p> One of the big challenges she identifies is that succession is not happening early enough . Farmers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ action . <p> " Farming men are just holding on to it because they did n't get it till they were 60 . Then my son is n't going to get it till they 're 60 . Like give it over . Just because you hand over the legal title does n't mean that you can not farm . <p> " You can still farm , but farm maybe together . You step back a little bit and let the son or the daughter , whoever , come in a bit more , " she suggests . <p> We need women to step up a bit more to be recognised , but they need the confidence to do that . And I do n't think they 're going to get the confidence unless people start seeing them as potential <p> While 2026 is the UN International Year of the Woman Farmer , another concern is a continuing reluctance to leave a farm to a daughter because of the family name . <p> " I 've seen cases where , firstly , because they 're a woman , they 're not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ says , ' Daddy , give it to the daughter . I do n't want anything to do with it , ' still , the son gets it because of the name . " <p> She has a simple remedy for this -- name the farm . And I do n't think they 're going to get the confidence unless people start seeing them as potential , " she says . <p> A growing sense of entitlement is also something that is cropping up more and more in succession given how valuable the land asset is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ stepping away too . @@139199675 <h> Change is coming for Irish agriculture ; but there is still too much uncertainty over how it will be supported . <p> The Just Transition Commission of Ireland 's report , published last week , said that decisions taken in the area of agricultural reform , land use and environmental policy over the coming years will have consequences for generations . <p> The Commission said it believes " agriculture and land use are at a pivotal moment " . <p> The report follows close on the heels of the publication of the second phase of the Land Use Review , and echoes the central challenge highlighted in that document . It summarises that challenge : " Farmers and rural communities are being asked to respond simultaneously to climate change , biodiversity loss , changing market conditions and evolving societal expectations , while continuing to produce food and sustain livelihoods . " <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> While the Land Use Review posited pathways for Irish agriculture which would achieve the goals already outlined by legislation and policy , the Commission report concentrates on how the coming transition should be managed in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> It is notable that among the recommendations put forward by the Commission is one which asks the government to " provide clarity " on how the findings of the Land Use Review will inform future policy and decision-making . There was not much sign of that clarity in the government reaction to the land use review . <p> The review itself makes it clear that it is not an articulation of government policy , nor is it a new strategy in relation to land use . Minister for Agriculture Martin Heydon emphasised that landowners are , and will be , the ultimate decision makers under any changes to land use in Ireland . <p> This suggests that government is more likely to adopt a carrot , rather than a stick , approach . <p> The Commission report on a Just Transition in Agriculture seems to be in agreement with this strategy . While it says that a national dialogue to develop a shared long-term vision for Irish agriculture needs to be " urgently " convened , the rest of its key recommendations are about how climate , socioeconomic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ changes farmers make can be rewarded . <p> It recommends that long-term , accessible and equitable funding mechanisms be established to support farmers and landowners undertaking climate action , nature restoration and generational land-use change and that policies provide fair and adequate financial recognition for the delivery of climate , biodiversity and ecosystem benefits . <p> On the market side , it recommends that government and stakeholders work together to strengthen market demand , develop routes to market and reduce barriers to diversification in agriculture . It also recommends that governance , regulatory and market frameworks support fair carbon-farming arrangements . <p> There are also recommendations around the future of farming aimed at increasing gender equality across agriculture and generational renewal . It says targeted supports for young farmers and measures to improve access to land for new entrants are needed as well as to ensure equitable access and participation throughout the sector . <p> Overall , the Commission states that the transition must be implemented in a way that creates opportunities for decent incomes , good working conditions and thriving rural economies . <p> The report is also clear @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The geographic clustering of farm types , where dairy is concentrated in the south and east of the country , and extensive beef and sheep operations more common in the west , mean that an unplanned transition could hit different areas of the country unevenly . The Commission notes that while current scenarios to reduce emissions from agriculture concentrate on technological and management measures -- citing the Teagasc Marginal Abatement Cost Curve ( MACC ) -- some scenarios acknowledge a reduction in beef cattle numbers while the dairy herd size increases . <p> A generation worth of data on cattle numbers in Ireland suggests that the " unplanned transition " is already well underway . <p> Ireland is almost unique in major European Union agricultural economies in that the country has managed to maintain overall cattle numbers at a fairly constant level over the last 30 years ( see Figure 1 ) . <p> However , that maintenance of that overall number hides the changes which have occurred within farming . Between 2005 and 2025 the number of dairy cows in Ireland increased by 50% to 1.5m , growth which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and which is unique in the EU ( see Figure 2 ) . <p> CAP <p> The Commission says that amendments to the current payments system , which have been proposed under reforms to the Common Agricultural Policy , provide an opportunity to improve how payments are distributed and their applicability . <p> It says that a support system that recognises additional competing demands for the land could ensure that in addition to food production , farmers are rewarded for ecosystem services , carbon sequestration , good water quality and the provision of quality habitats through longer-term , reliable payments . <p> On the thorny issue of what constitutes an " active farmer " , the Commission says that support for active farmers " must encompass the broad range of farmer experience that sustains many economic activities across rural communities in Ireland . Any definition of " active " farmer should continue to recognise low intensity farming and include farmers providing ecosystem services and climate and nature friendly farm activities . <p> Overall , the Commission says that while direct income support will remain important for farm viability , available @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ environmental , social and economic outcomes in line with the principles of a just transition . <p> There is an emphasis in the report from the Commission on the need for certainty in supports for farmers over the long term to ensure participation in the transition . <p> It says : " Long-term confidence will depend on whether climate action , land-use change and nature restoration are supported through fair funding arrangements , reliable income opportunities and policies that provide certainty over the long term . " <p> Comment <p> While the Just Transition Commission report makes more palatable reading than the recent Land Use Review , it does point to the same direction of travel for Ireland 's agricultural industry ; one where the land and the farmers who are custodians of it will have to deal with increasing demands over the coming years and decades . <p> While the report does n't directly highlight it , it seems from the data available that Ireland has been slow , when compared to our European neighbours , in grasping the thorny issue of land use and agricultural change . This @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been avoided , rather that it has been delayed . The Land Use Review notes that the delay could mean " more costly and drastic action " is needed in the longer term . <p> While Government says that final decisions on land use will remain with farmers and landowners , those groups have shown over many years that they react positively to well-designed incentive schemes . <p> The Just Transition report makes the crucial point that incentives for changes in land use must provide certainty over the long-term . Providing that certainty is the job of policymakers . If a farmer is being asked to change how they manage their land , or how intensively they farm , then they will need certainty over future sustainable income that runs over decades and generations , not over years , or even months , as we currently see in some cases . <p> So , yes , there are changes coming to Ireland 's agricultural system , but the immediate change that is needed is one in government and EU policy which will lock-in supports for the long-term and allow farmers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ over what the future could bring . <h> Email address not recognised <p> If you would like to speak to a member of our team , please call us on 01-4199525 . <h> Link sent to your email address <p> We have sent an email to your address . " <p> AR7E-2026 is led by Dr Yvonne Firing from the UK 's National Oceanography Centre , and comprises a team of scientists from institutions based in the UK , Norway , Belgium , Portugal , Switzerland and Greece . <p> The project is the first Marine Institute research vessel survey to receive funding through the Aquarius project , a four-year Horizon Europe-funded programme led by the Marine Institute that aims to provide access to research infrastructure to aid projects focused on marine and freshwater ecosystem sustainability . <p> The Marine Institute noted that the expedition is " particularly timely " as it contributes scientific knowledge from areas beyond national jurisdiction , meaning the vast regions of ocean lying outside any @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ international community begins implementing the landmark UN High Seas Treaty on Biodiversity Beyond National Jurisdiction , high-quality observations from these waters will play an increasingly important role in understanding , conserving and sustainably managing marine biodiversity , " said the organisation . @@139275794 <h> Ireland 's Greenest Places : Despite backlash in some political circles against the green agenda , action on the ground tells a different story <p> You have one week to nominate a person or place doing good work to restore nature and fight climate change here at home . Whether a nomination for an individual " net-zero hero " or for a suburb , village , town or community , this small act of recognition could make a big difference in time . <p> There may be a backlash in some political circles against the green agenda but increasing action on the ground tells a different story . Most people are still really worried about climate change . They want to see it addressed and are often willing to play their own active part in delivering solutions . <p> The evidence of that activism is all around us . Each year more and more Irish farmers are looking to see how they can use less synthetic fertiliser , to save costs of course but also to protect their local environment . Each month some 5,000 homes are putting up @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ same dual motives . Each week voluntary groups are also heading out on local community clean-ups , armed with nothing but a picker and a refuse sack , this time motivated by nothing but love for their own area . <p> Every nomination category is important , but the Best Suburb category is particularly interesting to me . It matters because about half of our homes are in the suburbs . It is hard to define the exact number because the boundaries are rarely clear between the city , suburb and countryside . This is particularly the case in Ireland , where so much one-off housing is dotted along country roads like a winding suburban estate , or where we have so much low-density housing right close to town . <p> Car dependency is one of the big drawbacks of our suburban living but anyone who has grown up in the suburbs also knows that they can be a great place to raise a family , where you can have good neighbours and a strong sense of community . There is often also a real connection to nature , thanks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ open skies and access to local rivers and streams , which retain a sense of place . <p> If you had to judge whether all this new green activity was really happening , what would you look for ? <p> Well you might start at the primary school gate , noting all the green flags , which reflect a commitment to prepare the next generation for the big challenges they will face . As the centre points of our community , you might also notice one of the 526 safe routes to school projects that have started in the past five years . That has been matched by the efforts of parents to make it safe again for their kids to walk and cycle to school , trying to allow children freely explore their own neighbourhood , which is one of the biggest things we 've lost in recent decades . <p> A second check might be to see if the suburb contains one of the thousand sustainable energy communities established across Ireland over the past 10 years . It has not been easy to get big projects up and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . However , every one of those communities have smart local people working together to try and accelerate the switch away from fossil fuels . It is going to take two or three decades to make the transition happen , but with such commitment we are well and truly on our way . <p> A third sense might come from visiting the local church . Three years ago the Irish Catholic bishops decided that 30 per cent of all church grounds would be returned to nature by 2030 . Things started happening very quickly in Clare , where 24 parishes have already acted and similar projects are now spreading across the country . It would be great if local authorities or State agencies were willing to set such a target for the public land they hold in trust for us all . <p> That would build on what local community groups are already doing . For example , in Tallaght they have developed real expertise in planting out pocket forests or digging in new wildlife ponds ; in Ballymun they have a particular gr ? for circular economy thinking ; @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Sam Maguire for urban regeneration planning . <p> Last , you might look at what local sports clubs are doing and see if the suburb has its own street feast celebration , which 1,200 communities held in May , recognising that promoting sustainability is as much a cultural as a physical thing . <p> It is for that cultural reason that it makes sense to take the small effort to nominate a person or place , where you see good things happening . I hope you might in particular nominate a suburb , however you may define it , in recognition of the good work that is going on , and to give us all some hope in this uncertain and insecure world , which we need to change one street at a time . <p> Eamon Ryan is chair of the European Commission 's housing advisory board . He is former Green Party leader and ex-minister for the environment . He is a judge on Ireland 's Greenest Places .

@@139299050 <h> Butterflies may be more colourful but moths bring their own special magic <p> Could you please recommend some garden plants that are moth friendly ? I 'd love to encourage more of them to visit the garden , they 're such magical little things . Mary F , Co Carlow <p> While there 's an abundance of information readily available to gardeners on creating a butterfly-friendly garden , that 's not the case when it comes to moths . Presumably this boils down to the simple reason of them being less visible and less colourful , but it 's a shame . As you say , they bring their own special magic while they 're also often more efficient as pollinators than bees . <p> But while Ireland is home to more than 1,500 species of moth , many are under threat as a result of a complex combination of factors including habitat loss , changes in land use , pollution , climate change , and the use of herbicides and insecticides . <p> To encourage them in your garden , create the kinds of sheltered , flower-rich habitats @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ using a mix of native species such as hawthorn , blackthorn , the guelder rose ( Viburnum opulus ) and hazel are a great start . So are native trees such as birch , willow , alder and rowan . Leaving pockets of wild grasses and native " weeds " such as yarrow , dandelion , clover , selfheal , bedstraw , groundsel , trefoil , scabious , ivy and knapweed will also help a lot . <p> It 's also worth noting that despite the common belief that moths are nocturnal , there are many that take to the wing by day . It 's just that their discreet colourings and markings typically provide valuable camouflage . 
@@139299622 <h> The times they are a-changin ' <p> Bob Dylan wrote and recorded a famous song in the ' 60s ; ' The times they are a-changin ' . Bob is still around today and if he had a hankerin ' to write a sequel to his iconic song , climate change would surely have to feature as the great time-changer . It might baffle Bob to find words to describe how some of his countrymen are still shouting loudly that climate change is a ' hoax ' . <p> No folks , climate change is here alright . In a way , it just crept up on us -- even though the warnings were sounding for the last 30 years . So , if the times are a-changin , we have to change with the times . <p> Climate change is bringing devastation by way of fire , floods and failures across the globe , but to use a weather metaphor ; ' it 's an ill wind that blows no good ' . You have often heard of ' the right man in the right place ' , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is the right country in the right place . <p> As I write this piece from my perch in Irishtown , we are past the second week of a heatwave , and the temperature is loitering around 26C today . This is the second such spell this summer . It is just so heavenly here close to the banks of Lough Owel . I observe droves of happy visitors mingling with locals and all welcomed and well looked after in Mullingar . You can expect a lot more of this . <p> Alejandro Saez Real of the World Meteorological Organization has said recently that continental tourism-reliant countries will be massively affected by extreme temperatures changing the trends of world tourism . Holiday makers will increasingly seek more comfortable temperatures rather than going for the hottest destinations . This is where we come in . <p> Even travel itself has become more fraught during summer months due to heatwaves , storms , flooding and wildfires . This summer has been quite different , most likely caused by climate change , and with that change comes a change in our behaviours too . <p> While any sun is typically met with an increase in outdoor activity , summer 2026 feels like unprecedented levels of barbecue planning and road trips -- why plan a foreign getaway when the Irish summer offers better conditions ? However , as this experience is so foreign to us , it comes with a few extra things to prepare for and be aware of in case of bad luck . <p> Of course , personal safety is important , and we 've surely all been aware of warnings around limiting our sun exposure to prevent medical issues such as sunstroke and skin cancer . As well as this , warnings around water safety increase as more people flock to pools and beaches to cool down , increasing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ exposure and water safety awareness are important , they are n't the only risks associated with a surge in temperatures . " From barbecues and garden fires to the impact of heat on vehicles , small issues can quickly escalate if precautions are n't taken . Taking a few practical steps can help people spot issues early or avoid them altogether . " <p> Taking care at home <p> Summertime hazards in and around the home are numerous when temperatures are on the rise . The recent wildfire in Killarney National Park , suspected to have been ignited by a disposable barbecue , serves as a reminder of the dangers of barbecues and fires in areas where the surrounding grass and shrubbery are dry , meaning it can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ avoiding using a barbecue in windy conditions , keeping them well away from buildings , and to fully cool any ashes before disposing of them carefully . It also cautions against bonfires and any other open flames in warm or dry weather , reminding people to never leave open flames unattended . <p> Other fire hazards within the home are plentiful in summer too , and flames could be caused by unexpected items . The insurer warns about mirrors , bottles , and other reflective items in particular , advising that they be moved from windowsills or sources of direct sunlight , as concentrated light reflected from them could spark a flame . <p> Overheating is another cause for concern , particularly when it comes to lithium-ion batteries , which can cause fires when running too hot . With this in mind , top up your oil and coolant levels before longer journeys . And while on the road for a longer trip , take more breaks to allow your engine to cool off . <p> Finally , watch out for signs of overheating in your vehicle . These include warning lights , rising temperature gauges , or steam under the bonnet .

@@139322005 <h> What is a ' fire cloud ' , and how have they worsened wildfires in Europe ? <p> When pyrocumulonimbus , or fire clouds , form , they can create winds that make the wildfires below spread even faster , and sometimes generate their own lightning , which can in turn ignite more fires further afield <p> Massive wildfires emit large amounts of energy and heat that rapidly rise in a smoke plume . When the fire plume rises high enough , low atmospheric pressure causes its air to cool , and the moisture condenses and forms a cloud . Such clouds can generate lightning strikes and windy conditions . File picture : Richard Capoulade/UGC via AP <p> As France and Spain battle some of the worst wildfires in recent history , experts have warned of an unprecedented phenomenon that could exacerbate the effects of the blazes : pyrocumulonimbus , or " fire clouds " . <p> Here is what you need to know about them . <p> What is a fire cloud ? <p> In a nutshell , pyrocumulonimbus , or " pyroCb " clouds , are gigantic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Massive wildfires emit large amounts of energy and heat that rapidly rise in a smoke plume . When the fire plume rises high enough , low atmospheric pressure causes its air to cool , and the moisture condenses and forms a cloud . Such clouds can generate lightning strikes and windy conditions . <p> If a wildfire is sufficiently large and intense , the smoke plume it generates can form a pyrocumulonimbus cloud , which can reach altitudes of 10 -- 15km and penetrate the stratosphere . Nasa calls them the " fire-breathing dragon of clouds " . <p> " It 's a full thunderstorm forming within the fire plume , " says Rick McRae , an adjunct professor from the University of New South Wales ' bushfire research group . " These things , they get up to roughly 10,000 cubic kilometres of the atmosphere , so they have a big impact . " <p> Why are these pyrocumulonimbus cloud formations so dangerous ? <p> When pyrocumulonimbus clouds form , they can create winds that make the wildfires below spread even faster , and sometimes generate their own lightning @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Experts say stronger wind gusts can create more unpredictable fire patterns and therefore make conditions more dangerous for firefighters battling the blazes on the ground . <p> " The cloud is confirmation that the fire is very dangerous , " says Mr McRae , who worked as a senior fire manager for 30 years . " The cloud is a confirmation that it 's appropriate that lots of people have been evacuated . " <p> How common are fire clouds ? <p> Australia started experiencing pyrocumulonimbus clouds at the beginning of the century and experts say it appears they are becoming more common . <p> They also formed during massive wildfires in California in 2022 , but this could be a new situation for France . <p> If confirmed , this would be the first time a pyroCb has been observed in France . <p> " It 's the first time France has had to explicitly consider the concept of a pyroCb , " says McRae . <p> Why are these clouds occurring more frequently ? <p> The climate crisis is creating more dangerous conditions for wildfires globally @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of pyrocumulonimbus clouds . <p> " It 's not just as simple as turning the temperature dial up . It 's full-on climate change , " says Mr McRae . " It 's changes in the atmosphere , changes in the surface weather , changes in a lot of things . " <p> Experts say climate models indicate these trends will increase in severity in the future . <p> The wildfires in Europe have happened during peak tourist season in a region that has been hit by successive heatwaves , with woodland areas dried out from a lack of rain since May . <p> Weather forecasters warn another heatwave could hit France from Tuesday , with temperatures reaching 40C in some areas .

@@139322181 <h> The importance of understanding and accepting our feelings <p> Many factors underpin this crisis such as decreased social connection and sedentary screen-based lifestyles . Photograph : Getty <p> We are living in a mental health crisis with people experiencing higher rates of depression and anxiety than ever before . A 2026 study in the Lancet showed a 95 per cent increase in mental health conditions since 1990 , with young people aged 18-29 particularly affected . <p> The next article looks at taking constructive action in the face of challenge <p> In addition , we live in particularly challenging times of international wars , climate breakdown , political insecurity and polarisation in society . This is having a big impact on mental health , with more people worried about the future . In another Lancet study , 84 per cent of young people reported being moderately or very worried about climate change , while 75 per cent reported finding the future frightening . <p> This summer we have a war in the Gulf threatening fuel shortages and economic chaos , and Europe has already experienced three record-breaking heatwaves with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ loss . <p> Most worryingly is the sense that this is only the start , with increased problems to come . <p> In a four-part series , I explore how we positively manage our mental health in the face of personal and global crisis so we can continue to respond creatively and humanely in the face of stress and challenges . <p> In this first article I look at the importance of understanding and accepting our feelings . <h> Acknowledging feelings <p> " The only pain that can be avoided is the pain that comes from trying to avoid pain . " -- RD Lang <p> People will often go great lengths to avoid their vulnerable feelings such as fear , sadness or jealousy . Sometimes this is out of a belief that they should be " strong " or due to a fear of losing control should their feelings take over . Many addictive behaviours such as alcohol , overeating , scrolling social media or frenetic work activity are simply a means to avoid , numb or distract from uncomfortable feelings . Sometimes , people mask or repress their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or burnout and many people inappropriately take out their feelings on those around them through contempt , anger or resentment . <p> Many mental health problems are the result of stuck or unprocessed emotions . Depression is often unacknowledged feelings of sadness and grief and obsessive anxiety is sometimes unexpressed fear held in the body . <h> Feeling your feelings <p> When supporting people dealing with distress and trauma the most important therapeutic response is to empathically validate and understand their emotions and feelings . The healing process of counselling is primarily about compassionate listening to help another person tease out and articulate their thoughts , feelings and needs . <p> Sometimes the most important step is to simply " feel your feelings " . In my own work as a therapist , often the significant therapeutic moment is when a person expresses a previously unacknowledged emotion for the first time . A depression can lift when a person expresses their underlying grief and loss through tears and crying . Noticing a hidden anger can help shift resentment and give space for forgiveness . Acknowledging real fear and expressing it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> One of the strengths of the many self-help mental health groups such as AA or Grow Mental Health or Aware is the normalising of vulnerable feelings . You can meet people who feel the same way as you . You are not alone or crazy , and you can express your feelings in a supportive space . Interestingly , as environmental problems proliferate , there is a growing number of support groups for people worried about climate breakdown such as climate psychology alliance , deep adaptation and parents for a future . <h> Managing your own mental health <p> Whether you attend counselling or a support group , there is a lot you can do for yourself to nurture your own mental health , especially when you are feeling stress or distress . In particular , I recommend the ritual of a " daily check-in " whereby you take a pause to acknowledge your feelings and thoughts . <p> This is usually best integrated into an existing relaxation routine you have such as walking the dog , sitting down for a morning coffee , or after daily meditation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ self-talk whereby you ask yourself a few questions such as : " How am I feeling right now ? " , " What is on my mind ? " , " What is worrying/ distressing me ? " <p> The key is ask these question compassionately and gently . Imagine you are a kind friend to yourself , genuinely inquiring after your wellbeing . Sometimes , it helps to tune in to your body to notice your feelings . Do you notice stress in your body ? Which parts ? How is your nervous system ? What are your stress levels ? <h> Using a journal <p> It can help to write down your answers in a journal , and this is something I particularly recommend . In your journal , you simply write down the answers to your self-talk questions : You might notice your vulnerable feelings : " At the moment , I am struggling with ... " or " I feel sad when ... " or " I notice anger in my body about ... " You can also note positive emotions such as " I was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lot to me when ... " Once you have written your journal , take a moment to review it -- remember to be self-compassionate and kind as you would to your best friend . <p> While not a replacement for counselling or the support of family and friends , therapeutic journal writing can have significant mental health benefits and several studies support this . <p> It allows people to safely express and contain difficult feelings , constructively reflect about problems and over time gain self-understanding and develop better coping skills . <p> It gives you space and time to compose and collect yourself for whatever daily challenges might be ahead . <p> This article is the first of a four-part series on supporting your mental health in the face of personal and global crisis . The next article looks at taking constructive action in the face of challenge .

@@139351977 <h> Were this summer 's heatwaves caused by El Ni ? o , or something even more terrifying ? <h> The scientist who first warned about climate change now says he was too optimistic - and this summer suggests he may be right <p> Eamon Ryan <p> Tue Jul 28 2026 - 06:00 * 4 MIN READ <p> Beachgoers rest on the beach of Moutchic in Lacanau , France , as clouds of smoke rise into the sky . Photograph : Maximilien LAMY/AFP via Getty Images <p> The climate system on which we depend for everything is breaking down before our eyes . The scale and speed of what is happening is truly terrifying and requires an emergency response that jolts us out of our inaction . <p> Some scientists knew about the relevant atmospheric physics as far back as the 1850s . The oil industry also commissioned its own research in the 1970s , which made clear the risks -- a Harvard analysis found internal projections made by ExxonMobil from 1977-2003 were " shockingly " accurate -- but the industry decided to keep the information to itself . It @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ when the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) was established and James Hansen , director of the Nasa Goddard Institute for Space Studies , set out the principles of climate science in a US Senate hearing . <p> Those key principles soon became incontrovertible and verified by every credible test . However , the timing was still somewhat uncertain . The assumptions involved in climate modelling made it hard to judge exactly how our complex planetary systems would work in this fast-changing world . Unfortunately , it now seems that the models underestimated rather than exaggerated the problem . <p> Real-time measurements mean we are no longer just modelling what might happen . The latest data shows we are experiencing an acceleration in the rate of climate change . <p> Extreme weather events supercharged by the rise in temperatures are impacting on biosystems in a way that leads to the release of further emissions -- such as the recent forest fires happening right across the globe . There is a risk it could become a runaway phenomenon , and we will only know if we 've crossed a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ involved . <p> Hansen was one of the first to raise the alarm about this acceleration . Nearly four years ago , he was lead author in a paper thatargued the IPCC 's assessment of the scientific literature for temperature increase was too optimistic and had underestimated the climate sensitivity of our planet . It presented findings that showed how our oceans , ice sheets and cloud systems were already changing in response to the existing increase in temperatures , which in turn is altering how much solar energy is being retained on our planet , rather than reflected out into space . <p> It also argued that a recent reduction in sulphur emissions from ships ' engines and power stations -- which had been introduced for good health reasons -- was having inadvertent consequences . Those particles had a short-term effect in reflecting some solar energy away from Earth , which masked the longer-term energy imbalance that was taking hold . <p> The analysis was attacked at the time by the usual climate deniers and even questioned by some fellow scientists , but is now looking increasingly prescient . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , showing the temperature rise had almost doubled in the last decade . <p> It is hard to know how much of the deadly heatwaves this summer should be attributed to this acceleration or to the arrival of another extreme El Ni ? o event . The worst impacts of El Ni ? o are expected later this year , and we are now seeing a dramatic increase in ocean temperatures , which is profoundly impacting marine ecosystems . <p> This climate acceleration will bring us into dangerous territory , which we might have expected to arrive in our children 's lifetime and not our own . We are already experiencing shocks to our food systems , which will only become more widespread . The futuristic charts showing parts of France and Spain becoming burnt out are now looking like the weather maps we see today . <p> The political system should be on red alert and doing everything possible to defend us against this existential threat . Instead , governments are retreating from commitments entered into only 10 years ago , following the signing of the Paris climate agreement @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climate science . You would have to be very thick not to get the basic facts . But unfortunately political science trumps climate science at every turn . Unless it is clear that public demand for climate action is greater than the case for building more ring roads , bringing in more gas and buying more cattle , then that 's what we will continue to do . <p> There can be no giving up because it is never too late to reduce emissions . We have to adapt to what is happening but still focus on reducing greenhouse gases -- because no amount of adaptation will work if the underlying cause is left unaddressed . <p> We can not afford to be frozen by fear , and perhaps more than anything else , we need the public demand for climate action to now accelerate , so we become a tipping point for the good instead .

@@139352297 <p> A British startup is working to solve the challenges of cost and scale for the nuclear energy industry by using highly efficient high-temperature turbines used by gas-fired power plants and jet engines . Emerging from stealth , Nuclear Turbines promises to leverage British expertise in engineering and design to build smaller , affordable nuclear energy reactors . <p> As countries grapple with demands for energy and increasing instances of extreme weather events due to climate change , nuclear energy is rapidly becoming the approach of choice to balance the two . With no carbon emissions and on-demand availability of energy , nuclear power is poised to make a major comeback to the energy scene . <p> Interesting Engineering has previously reported on new innovative approaches being deployed in the fission energy sector , where modular and smaller-scale reactors are being tested to meet energy demands . Jeremy Owston , formerly an engineer at BAE Systems and a co-founder at Nuclear Turbines , however , believes there is a fundamental problem in how the nuclear energy sector approaches energy production . <h> Flipping the script <p> According to Owston , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ decides how it will convert the generated heat into electricity . At Nuclear Turbines , and his co-founder , Tim Abram , a professor at The University of Manchester , are replacing the steam turbine systems with those used in gas-fired power plants . <p> Nuclear power plants use steam turbines because the reactors produce low-temperature heat ( 572 Fahrenheit , 300 degrees Celsius ) . The nuclear reactor design requires the use of large tanks , boilers , heat exchangers , and cooling systems that are meant to handle high-pressure water safely . The end result is a big infrastructure setup that is expensive to build and highly inefficient . <p> In sharp contrast , gas turbines handle high-temperature heat and convert them directly into power . Used in jet engines too , these turbines are compact , extremely efficient , and cost-effective even at small scales . However , these turbines can not be used directly in nuclear reactors , since they can operate at high temperatures of above 572 Fahrenheit ( 300 degrees Celsius ) . <h> What Nuclear Turbines is doing differently <p> Using first principles @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ system design that can generate electricity at the lowest cost . Much like a commercial gas turbine , the Nuclear Turbines reactor directly heats the compressed air before it is returned to the turbine at high temperatures to generate electricity directly . <p> The radical change in thinking has also brought about a radical change in the size and complexity of the overall system . Unlike other radical changes that usually require technology to mature , the Nuclear Turbines team used off-the-shelf components , giving better control over costs and rapid advancement of their innovation too . <p> While high-temperature turbines typically use graphite , which combusts when exposed to air , the team ensured that they replace it with novel materials to bypass the use of graphite completely . <p> " This is the first time I 've seen an SMR design that genuinely solves the economic and sustainability equation , and it 's at a time when market dynamics , government ambition and regulatory reform are creating unprecedented opportunity , " said Jordan Billiald , Principal at IQ Capital , who led a foundational funding round where the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) . <p> " Nuclear Turbines is perfectly positioned to capture that opportunity and to make sure the UK is not left out of the global nuclear race . " <p> Ameya is a science writer based in Hyderabad , India . A Molecular Biologist at heart , he traded the micropipette to write about science during the pandemic and does not want to go back . He likes to write about genetics , microbes , technology , and public policy . 
@@139375059 <h> Intrepid Travel supports vital reforestation in East Africa <p> In support of reforestation efforts , Intrepid will plant 20,000 native seedlings to help restore 15 acres of degraded forest in Kenya 's Rift Valley . <p> Intrepid Travel , a global leader in responsible travel for over 30 years , aims to reforest 15 acres of land in Kenya with the support of travellers , a local NGO , and the Kenyan government . <p> In an ambitious initiative to combat deforestation , the tour operator has received a licence from the Kenya Forest Service to plant 20,000 trees across 15 acres of land in the Mau Forest in Kenya . The initiative forms part of local efforts to achieve the Kenyan government 's goal of planting 15 billion trees by 2032 . <p> The Mau Forest is the vital catchment providing water to the western part of Kenya . It is also an important contributor to the Maasai Mara Game Reserve and the Serengeti National Park because the Mara River , which flows through these wildlife reserves , arises from the South-Western Mau Forest . <p> From 2027 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be able to visit the site to learn more about the historical context contributing to the degradation and then plant a tree or water one , helping to maintain the saplings and contribute to the ecosystem they explore on their trip . <p> Through this initiative and using native saplings provided by NGO Rhino Arc , Intrepid and its travellers will contribute to vital reforestation and help combat biodiversity loss and climate change in the region . The initial licence is for five years ; Intrepid plans to plant 5,000 trees each season to reach its targeted 20,000 trees . The adventure travel company hopes to expand this initiative at the end of the current licence by moving into a new tract of land to repeat the process . <p> " Kenya 's rich biodiversity is a vital part of the African and global ecosystem . We can enjoy all it has to offer but we also have an obligation to protect and preserve this land . Our custodianship of these 15 acres is just the beginning -- we have already planted over 4,000 trees on the way to achieving @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 20,000-tree goal , " says Samuel Karani , General Manager , East Africa , at Intrepid Travel . 
@@139381891 <h> Fires in Slievenamon , Connemara and Killarney show our hills are on life support <p> We should invest in mountains like we invest in schools , roads and hospitals , and acknowledge Slievenamon , along with all other hills , has been sick for some time and needs help if it is going to recover <p> Members of the Irish Air Corps provide aerial support in tackling a major wildfire as it continues to burn at Slievenamon in Co Tipperary . <p> The terrifying sight of flames approaching your house while you hastily gather belongings in the small hours of the night , or of familiar vistas transformed into a wall of smoke , are reminders of our rapidly changing world . <p> This month , it was the turn of people living around Slievenamon in Co Tipperary , to join communities in south-west France , the hills outside Madrid and the Canadian province of Ontario to taste the acrid smoke in the back of their throats . <p> Those experiencing this will no doubt be in shock but they should not be surprised . Climate change , ecological @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ trifecta . <p> Plumes of smoke from the wildfires on Slievenamon . Picture : Roz Purcell/Instagram . <p> In fact , according to the european forest fires information service , the area that has burned in Ireland is so far below the average measured between 2006 and 2025 . By July 22 , their figures state that 2,388 hectares were burned , nearly half of the burned area in 2025 and far lower than the peak recorded of 16,724ha in 2011 . Fires , in other words -- even big , dangerous ones -- have been with us for some time . <p> Before the fire broke out last week , Slievenamon was a sick and neglected mountain . Ecologically speaking , it was on life support . In 2004 , the National Parks and Wildlife Service ( NPWS ) wrote that the area , which is legally protected as a Natural Heritage Area , " shows evidence of disturbance from burning and grazing " , including old and new fires . <p> No more recent surveys are available . Nevertheless , we do know from a report submitted to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in bad condition : drained , cut out for turf , overgrazed by sheep and over-run by invasive species . <p> The lower slopes of Slievenamon , like many of our uplands , are ringed with monoculture plantations of tightly packed , and flammable , conifers . Gorse , a flammable bush , regenerates well after fire , exacerbating the issue further . <p> This is all directly related to the fire -- because if the land were a combination of wet bog and native forest , the flames would not have taken such a hold . <p> A helicopter dropping water on the fire that spread to an adjacent Coillte forest after changes in wind direction . The wildfire that is burning across Slievenamon in Co Tipperary split into three separate fires . Picture : Eamonn Farrell / RollingNews.ie <p> We saw this effect in 2021 when a devastating fire in Killarney National Park swept over the desiccated open moor but stopped when it reached the oak forest . This fire could have been an inflection point . <p> So might the one in Connemara in 2017 which consumed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from Galway City , 30km away . Or so might the hundreds of other fires which have caused incalculable damage over the past decades , including in national parks and nature reserves . <p> The Irish countryside should not be flammable . The bogs and uplands have only become so through centuries of deforestation , cutting away of peat , over-grazing with livestock , especially sheep , land drainage and inappropriate forestry . <p> Those who recklessly start fires , maliciously or otherwise are unlikely to face sanction and even in the unlikely event that a prosecution is secured , the fines are not much of a deterrent . <p> The State has recognised this , with nature and biodiversity minister Christopher O'Sullivan overseeing legislative change , including reform of the Wildlife Act and a proposed new law for national parks . However , this process is extremely slow ( the government first committed to passing a law to provide legal backing to national parks in 2002 ) . <p> Meanwhile , the approach to how our land is used remains stubbornly resistant to reform . This is not for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ A review of land use was included in the programme for government in 2020 . Phase 1 of this review , which focused on evidence gathering , was published in 2023 . <p> A second phase was completed in April 2025 but was only published in July of this year . This report says it is a " framework " to support decisions around how land is used but the Government felt compelled to state , repeatedly in the report , that it was " not an articulation of Government policy " . <p> The fires that annually devastate landscapes must be seen as a symptom of the inability of politicians , most notably within the three biggest political parties , to articulate to farmers and foresters what the future for their sectors looks like while complying with all the environmental laws , regulations and policy objectives that have piled up over the last three decades . <p> Land lies at the intersection of culture , food production , subsidies and imagined notions of rural Ireland : how the landscape should look and which activities are considered acceptable and which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the 1970s , have grown up thinking hills with sheep and conifer plantations are normal and healthy when they are far from it . Vested interests in the farming and forestry sectors have fought hard to maintain the status quo . <p> Politicians will have to face up to these contradictions eventually . The good news is that there are options . The fire on Slievenamon will be extinguished . New growth will reappear . We could acknowledge this mountain , along with all the others , has been sick for some time and needs help if it is going to recover . <p> Drains can be blocked , bogs can be wetted , native trees and forests can be established , monocultures can be replaced with diversity , invasive species can be removed . We can invest in the mountain like we invest in schools , roads and hospitals . <p> This means allocating substantial funding for implementing the forthcoming Nature Restoration Plan and setting out a plan to transition farming and forestry to sustainable models . We can accelerate the passing of laws so there are stiffer fines @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ prosecutions . <p> The fire on Slievenamon will not be the last , but it should be the one that forces a rethink on how we are managing the hills . <p> P ? draic Fogarty is an ecologist and the author of Whittled Away : Ireland 's vanishing nature

( Photograph : courtesy of Construct Ireland ) <p> Wed Jul 29 2026 - 20:18 * 2 MIN READ <p> The conclusion of the latest report from the Climate Change Advisory Council ( CCAC ) that Ireland is well off track in upgrading homes and commercial buildings to be more energy efficient comes as little surprise . As with elsewhere in climate policy , progress is being made but at far too slow a pace . <p> Emissions from homes fell by 5 per cent last year , but much of this was due to a milder winter which meant heating was not turned on as often . Looking out to 2030 , both the residential and commercial sectors are set to miss their targets by substantial amounts . <p> The council underlines the need for a rapid acceleration in the retrofitting of homes and the take up of heat pumps @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ electricity . Around 90 per cent of heating demand is still met by fossil fuels -- oil and gas . It also makes the case for greater use of solar energy , including in apartments . <p> In terms of retrofitting , this was completed -- to a Ber B2 standard or better -- on 84,270 homes by the end of last year , compared to a 2025 target of 120,000 . The total number of heat pumps installed between 2019 and 2025 was 18,580 against a target of 45,000 . <p> New Government incentives have increased demand , but the significant cost of the retrofitting standard needed to install a heat pump and the upheaval of getting the work done is still holding back many households . The Government is also behind in retrofitting its own housing stock . <p> The CCAC makes the case for accelerating progress , in the context of meeting Ireland 's climate targets . It urges the Government to publish the delayed National Building Renovation Plan , which is intended to provide the strategy for getting from where Ireland is now to where it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ State 's climate strategy , the targets are there but a realistic implementation plan is not . And this lack of clarity goes right to the core of the strategy , which requires the development of extensive offshore wind farms . <p> In terms of households , a recent Economic and Social Research Institute ( ESRI ) report raised some vital points . It highlighted how the Government faces a trade off in terms of allocating scarce building resources to retrofitting , working on new homes and national infrastructure . It made the case for focusing retrofitting supports on households with the highest emissions . And for considering interim measures , such as moving houses on home heating oil to gas boilers which can in time use more sustainable fuels . <p> The Government 's strategy needs to be ambitious but also realistic . It needs to outline clearly how the trade-offs will be addressed and how it will incentivise households to do what is needed . The EU- mandated target of getting rid of fossil fuel boilers by 2040 looks a long way off .

@@139399886 <h> EDITORIAL : An Expanding Calamity <p> Amid all the alarming reports of fires , floods and heat domes around the world in recent weeks and months there was a report that , in only a slightly amusing way , took the breath away . <p> And that concerned camels in the desert wastes of Africa . The camels , it would appear , are having an ever tougher time in trying to bear rising heat . Well , if the camels are having a hard time of it , what about us humans ? Suffice it to say all too many humans are having a hard and harder time of it too . <h> Sign up to The Irish Echo Newsletter <p> Sign up today to get daily , up-to-date news and views from Irish America . <p> Back in the early months of the year , March specifically , the first big fire report of 2026 came out of Nebraska . The " Morrill Fire " - named after one of the four Nebraska counties it scorched - raced 70 miles across the state 's landscape in one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ modern Nebraska history , as well as one of the largest in United States history . <p> In the end , when it was finally contained on March 25th , the Morrill Fire had burned 643,361 acres . This page checked around for a comparative area and came up with County Roscommon . The Morrill Fire would have burned every square foot of Roscommon - plus an additional 15,000 acres or so . <p> That was just one of the fires in Nebraska this year , one of the fires in the United States , one of the fires in North America , one of the fires in the world . <p> Meanwhile , parts of Texas were being ravaged by floods , ice sheets and glaciers across the globe continued to retreat , and the oceans continued to rise . It might well be the case , indeed probably is , that we are facing the greatest challenge to the nature of our existence , indeed our actual existence , in recorded human history . <p> Last week there was an astonishing photo on the front page of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ deckchairs on the beach in Lacanau , southwestern France . They are looking across a stretch of water at a wildfire . The two appear relaxed , but it is hard to imagine that they actually were . The fire had the appearance of an exploding atomic bomb . This could well be the news picture ( taken by Getty Images ) of the year . Then again it was taken in July and " fire season " is now a twelve month phenomenon . So more to come . <p> The fires that have threatened major European cities such as Madrid and Bordeaux have brought home to Europeans the fact that they are inhabiting a widening continental fire zone that pays no heed to national boundaries . And national leaders are sounding the alarm and seeking new ways to respond . As well they should . <p> Climate change , one way or another , is everywhere . Off Europe 's continental landmass there have been fires in Britain and Ireland . Ireland is in a state of drought and there have been fires in various counties . One @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Tipperary . It burned gorse before spreading to forestry plantations . <p> Thankfully it is not close to the scale of infernos in Europe or North America , but the fire on the iconic mountain has been a stark reminder that nowhere is safe or immune to the effects of shifting climate . <p> Across the Atlantic in North America fire continues to ravage the Canadian boreal forests . There have been fires in Northern Minnesota and now there are raging infernos in both Washington State and Oregon . We are reminded , amid this hell , of the extraordinary courage of firefighters and other emergency workers . <p> All these fires are producing " filthy " smoke , as President Trump would describe it . And , indeed , millions of people have been breathing smoke in recent weeks as the summer of ' 26 unleashes a pall covering vast swathes of the western hemisphere . <p> We will not even bother to focus here on the deliberate burning of the Amazon rainforest . That is another story altogether . <p> Most fires are caused by lightning strikes or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ up being the same : bad , very bad . <p> It would be appropriate here to say that it is time for all of us to get our act together on climate change and its encroaching devastating effects . But that would suggest that we actually have an act to get together . We do n't . <p> The pace of change is far greater than humanity 's seeming ability to react in a coordinated way . And with the United States not being even an official recognizer of global climate change the prospects for the remainder of 2026 , and the years to follow are , to say the least , not encouraging .
